It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. It started after the robbery at that festival job I had — that was about two years and three months ago, I think. I don’t remember the exact day off the top of my head, but I kind of measure it from that, because before then I still did those things pretty normally. And after that, over time, it was like the interest just drained out of them. So yeah… about two years and three months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it “sad” exactly all the time. It’s more like there’s this low, sour feeling underneath everything most days. Like fear and guilt mixed together, even when nothing is actually happening and I should be fine.

I’ll be sitting at home or doing something normal, and there’s still this… heaviness, I guess. Not always crying-sad, but not okay either. More like my body and my mind are acting like something is wrong, and then I feel bad about feeling that way, like I should’ve handled things better or I shouldn’t still be like this. It’s there in the background a lot.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really… or at least not the way I used to. Sometimes if I make myself go, there might be a tiny moment where I’m like, “Okay, this is fine,” but it doesn’t really feel good in that deeper way anymore.

Like dance class used to make me feel like myself, like I could just be in my body and enjoy moving. Hiking used to feel peaceful, and cooking new recipes felt fun, like a little project. Even game night used to be easy and funny. Now they all just feel like things I’m supposed to do because they used to matter to me. I’ll go through the motions, but mostly I’m waiting for it to be over, or I’m thinking about how much effort it took to get there. So… no, I don’t really get much pleasure from them now.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… sometimes. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it, it just sort of barges in. Like if I hear a cash box snap shut — that sharp metal sound — it can bring me right back really fast. Or if I’m in a kitchen and somebody comes up behind me too suddenly, even if they’re just reaching for something or trying to get past me, my whole body reacts before I can think.

It’s like for a second I’m not fully where I am anymore. I can hear the voices again, like the same tone and the same kind of panic, and my stomach just drops. It feels very immediate, like it’s happening right then, even though I know it’s not. And then I feel kind of embarrassed because nothing is actually happening in that moment, but inside it feels like it is. So yes… not constantly, but more than I want to.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not usually out of absolutely nowhere, I don’t think. It’s more like… something sets it off, even if it’s a small thing that maybe wouldn’t mean anything to someone else.

Like I said, the cash box sound is a big one — that snap when it shuts, that sharp metal kind of sound. Or if I’m in a kitchen and someone comes up behind me too fast, even if they’re not doing anything wrong, it’s like my body doesn’t wait to figure that out. It just reacts. And then the robbery comes back really fast, like I can hear the voices again and my stomach drops, and for a second it feels like it’s happening right then.

So I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there peacefully and suddenly it appears with no reason at all. It’s more that the reason can be really quick or ordinary, and then I’m back in it before I’ve even had time to think.

I also see friends less and feel like my life has gotten smaller around what feels safe.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the robbery at that festival job about two years and three months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it in a clear way, like hours or patterns or anything. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know if it’s enough to call it a real sleep problem.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed that I don’t always feel very rested, but I also can’t say for sure whether that’s because my sleep is bad or because I’m just… worn down in general. I haven’t been paying attention to it the way maybe I should. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, but I also wouldn’t say sleep has felt completely normal either.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the robbery at the festival job, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it, it just sort of barges in. Like if I hear a cash box snap shut — that sharp metal sound — it can bring me right back really fast. Or if I’m in a kitchen and somebody comes up behind me too suddenly, even if they’re just reaching for something or trying to get past me, my whole body reacts before I can think.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m not fully where I am anymore. I can hear the voices again, like the same tone and the same kind of panic, and my stomach just drops. It feels very immediate, like it’s happening right then, even though I know it’s not. And then I feel kind of embarrassed because nothing is actually happening in that moment, but inside it feels like it is. So yes… not constantly, but more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have memories or flashbacks of the robbery that are not triggered by anything specific?\n\nPatient: Not usually out of absolutely nowhere, I don’t think. It’s more like… something sets it off, even if it’s a small thing that maybe wouldn’t mean anything to someone else.\n\nLike I said, the cash box sound is a big one — that snap when it shuts, that sharp metal kind of sound. And then the robbery comes back really fast, like I can hear the voices again and my stomach drops, and for a second it feels like it’s happening right then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there peacefully and suddenly it appears with no reason at all. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. Maybe more than I like to admit.

It’s not always a clear thought like, “This was my fault,” but there’s this feeling that sits there most days, kind of underneath everything. Like a low, sour mix of fear and guilt, even when nothing bad is actually happening right then. I’ll be doing something normal and still feel like I’m carrying this heaviness around, like I should have done something different or handled it better somehow.

And I know, logically, maybe that doesn’t make sense. I can tell myself that. But it doesn’t really stop the feeling. It’s just there a lot of the time, kind of coloring the whole day. So yes, guilt is definitely part of it.

the same line over and over, then get stuck on small choices like what price to put down or which email to answer first.\n- key: S069\n  name: Intrusive_Symptoms\n  description: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: Sometimes a cash box snapping shut or somebody coming up behind me in a kitchen brings the robbery back so fast that I can hear the voices again and my stomach drops like it is happening right then.\n- key: S070\n  name: Trauma_Avoidance\n  description: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: I go out of my way to turn down festival jobs, evening deliveries, and certain shared kitchens because I do not want to think about that night or feel that panic start up.\n- key: S073\n  name: Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame\n  description: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.\n  manifestation: I keep telling myself I should have known better than to take that booking and that I failed the people helping me by not keeping them safe.\n- key: S074\n  name: Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State\n  description: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).\n  manifestation: Most days there is this low, sour mix of fear and guilt under everything, even when nothing bad is actually going on.\n- key: S075\n  name: Social_Detachment\n  description: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.\n  manifestation: I still answer friends sometimes, but when I am with them I feel like I am watching from far away and I do not know how to jump back into being close like before.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: Little things set me off now, like my mom asking about work or a helper moving too slow, and I hear myself snapping before I can stop it.\n- key: S078\n  name: Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior\n  description: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.\n  manifestation: A few times I have taken last-minute solo catering jobs in sketchy places just because I needed the money back, even though part of me knew it was not smart to go alone.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding And then the robbery comes back really fast, like I can hear the voices again and my stomach drops, and for a second it feels like it’s happening right then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there peacefully and suddenly it appears with no reason at all. Like a low, sour mix of fear and guilt, even when nothing bad is actually happening right then. I’ll be doing something normal and still feel like I’m carrying this heaviness around, like I should have done something different or handled it better somehow.\n\nAnd I know, logically, maybe that doesn’t make sense. I can tell myself that. But it doesn’t really stop the feeling. It’s just there a lot of the time, kind of coloring the whole day. So yes, guilt is definitely part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t noticed myself moving slowly in a way that I could point to, like someone watching me would say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if I’m just paying more attention to myself now. I’ll catch myself shifting around or not quite settling, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking. I… I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear thought like, “I’m going to hurt myself,” if that’s what you mean.

There are moments where I feel worn down or like I don’t want to deal with everything, but I’m not sure that’s the same as wishing I was dead. I don’t think I’ve been making plans or anything like that. It’s more… I get tired of feeling the way I feel, and then I feel guilty for even saying that out loud.

So, no, not in a direct way. But I guess I’m a little unsure where the line is between being really tired of things and what you’re asking.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I’ve kind of stopped caring about things I used to really enjoy. Like, not just one thing, but a bunch of things that used to feel like “me,” if that makes sense. Dance class, hiking, trying new recipes, even game night with people I like… they all just feel like chores now, like something sitting on a list that I keep avoiding or putting off. I know I used to look forward to them, and now I just… don’t. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. It started after the robbery at that festival job I had — that was about two years and three months ago, I think. I don’t remember the exact day off the top of my head, but I kind of measure it from that, because before then I still did those things pretty normally. And after that, over time, it was like the interest just drained out of them. So yeah… about two years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since you lost interest in those activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it “sad” exactly all the time. It’s more like there’s this low, sour feeling underneath everything most days. Like fear and guilt mixed together, even when nothing is actually happening and I should be fine.\n\nI’ll be sitting at home or doing something normal, and there’s still this… heaviness, I guess. Not always crying-sad, but not okay either. More like my body and my mind are acting like something is wrong, and then I feel bad about feeling that way, like I should’ve handled things better or I shouldn’t still be like this. It’s there in the background a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you still experience pleasure when you do engage in these activities, even if you don't look forward to them?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least not the way I used to. Sometimes if I make myself go, there might be a tiny moment where I’m like, “Okay, this is fine,” but it doesn’t really feel good in that deeper way anymore.\n\nLike dance class used to make me feel like myself, like I could just be in my body and enjoy moving. Hiking used to feel peaceful, and cooking new recipes felt fun, like a little project. Even game night used to be easy and funny. Now they all just feel like things I’m supposed to do because they used to matter to me. I’ll go through the motions, but mostly I’m waiting for it to be over, or I’m thinking about how much effort it took to get there. So… no, I don’t really get much pleasure from them now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything like that. I don’t think there’s been some huge change that I’ve noticed, like my clothes suddenly not fitting or people commenting on it.\n\nThere might be little ups and downs, but nothing where I’d say, “Yes, I lost a lot,” or “Yes, I gained a lot.” It’s just not something I’ve paid a ton of attention to compared to everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it in a clear way, like hours or patterns or anything. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know if it’s enough to call it a real sleep problem.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed that I don’t always feel very rested, but I also can’t say for sure whether that’s because my sleep is bad or because I’m just… worn down in general. I haven’t been paying attention to it the way maybe I should. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, but I also wouldn’t say sleep has felt completely normal either.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the robbery at the festival job, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it, it just sort of barges in. Like if I hear a cash box snap shut — that sharp metal sound — it can bring me right back really fast. Or if I’m in a kitchen and somebody comes up behind me too suddenly, even if they’re just reaching for something or trying to get past me, my whole body reacts before I can think.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m not fully where I am anymore. I can hear the voices again, like the same tone and the same kind of panic, and my stomach just drops. It feels very immediate, like it’s happening right then, even though I know it’s not. And then I feel kind of embarrassed because nothing is actually happening in that moment, but inside it feels like it is. So yes… not constantly, but more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have memories or flashbacks of the robbery that are not triggered by anything specific?\n\nPatient: Not usually out of absolutely nowhere, I don’t think. It’s more like… something sets it off, even if it’s a small thing that maybe wouldn’t mean anything to someone else.\n\nLike I said, the cash box sound is a big one — that snap when it shuts, that sharp metal kind of sound. I don’t think I’ve had that thought in that exact way, like, “I’d be better off dead.” That sounds very definite, and I don’t think my mind has gone there clearly.

I know I used to look forward to them, and now I just… don’t. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. It started after the robbery at that festival job I had — that was about two years and three months ago, I think. I don’t remember the exact day off the top of my head, but I kind of measure it from that, because before then I still did those things pretty normally. And after that, over time, it was like the interest just drained out of them. So yeah… about two years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since you lost interest in those activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it “sad” exactly all the time. It’s more like there’s this low, sour feeling underneath everything most days. Like fear and guilt mixed together, even when nothing is actually happening and I should be fine.\n\nI’ll be sitting at home or doing something normal, and there’s still this… heaviness, I guess. Not always crying-sad, but not okay either. More like my body and my mind are acting like something is wrong, and then I feel bad about feeling that way, like I should’ve handled things better or I shouldn’t still be like this. It’s there in the background a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you still experience pleasure when you do engage in these activities, even if you don't look forward to them?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least not the way I used to. Sometimes if I make myself go, there might be a tiny moment where I’m like, “Okay, this is fine,” but it doesn’t really feel good in that deeper way anymore.\n\nLike dance class used to make me feel like myself, like I could just be in my body and enjoy moving. Hiking used to feel peaceful, and cooking new recipes felt fun, like a little project. Even game night used to be easy and funny. Now they all just feel like things I’m supposed to do because they used to matter to me. I’ll go through the motions, but mostly I’m waiting for it to be over, or I’m thinking about how much effort it took to get there. So… no, I don’t really get much pleasure from them now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything like that. I don’t think there’s been some huge change that I’ve noticed, like my clothes suddenly not fitting or people commenting on it.\n\nThere might be little ups and downs, but nothing where I’d say, “Yes, I lost a lot,” or “Yes, I gained a lot.” It’s just not something I’ve paid a ton of attention to compared to everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it in a clear way, like hours or patterns or anything. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know if it’s enough to call it a real sleep problem.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed that I don’t always feel very rested, but I also can’t say for sure whether that’s because my sleep is bad or because I’m just… worn down in general. I haven’t been paying attention to it the way maybe I should. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, but I also wouldn’t say sleep has felt completely normal either.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the robbery at the festival job, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it, it just sort of barges in. Like if I hear a cash box snap shut — that sharp metal sound — it can bring me right back really fast. Or if I’m in a kitchen and somebody comes up behind me too suddenly, even if they’re just reaching for something or trying to get past me, my whole body reacts before I can think.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m not fully where I am anymore. I can hear the voices again, like the same tone and the same kind of panic, and my stomach just drops. It feels very immediate, like it’s happening right then, even though I know it’s not. And then I feel kind of embarrassed because nothing is actually happening in that moment, but inside it feels like it is. It’s more like… something sets it off, even if it’s a small thing that maybe wouldn’t mean anything to someone else.\n\nLike I said, the cash box sound is a big one — that snap when it shuts, that sharp metal kind of sound. Or if I’m in a kitchen and someone comes up behind me too fast, even if they’re not doing anything wrong, it’s like my body doesn’t wait to figure that out. It just reacts. And then the robbery comes back really fast, like I can hear the voices again and my stomach drops, and for a second it feels like it’s happening right then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there peacefully and suddenly it appears with no reason at all. It’s more that the reason can be really quick or ordinary, and then I’m back in it before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know if it’s enough to call it a real sleep problem.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed that I don’t always feel very rested, but I also can’t say for sure whether that’s because my sleep is bad or because I’m just… worn down in general. I haven’t been paying attention to it the way maybe I should. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, but I also wouldn’t say sleep has felt completely normal either.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the robbery at the festival job, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it, it just sort of barges in. Like if I hear a cash box snap shut — that sharp metal sound — it can bring me right back really fast. Or if I’m in a kitchen and somebody comes up behind me too suddenly, even if they’re just reaching for something or trying to get past me, my whole body reacts before I can think.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m not fully where I am anymore. I can hear the voices again, like the same tone and the same kind of panic, and my stomach just drops. It feels very immediate, like it’s happening right then, even though I know it’s not. And then I feel kind of embarrassed because nothing is actually happening in that moment, but inside it feels like it is. So yes… not constantly, but more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have memories or flashbacks of the robbery that are not triggered by anything specific?\n\nPatient: Not usually out of absolutely nowhere, I don’t think. It’s more like… something sets it off, even if it’s a small thing that maybe wouldn’t mean anything to someone else.\n\nLike I said, the cash box sound is a big one — that snap when it shuts, that sharp metal kind of sound. Or if I’m in a kitchen and someone comes up behind me too fast, even if they’re not doing anything wrong, it’s like my body doesn’t wait to figure that out. It just reacts. And then the robbery comes back really fast, like I can hear the voices again and my stomach drops, and for a second it feels like it’s happening right then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there peacefully and suddenly it appears with no reason at all. It’s more that the reason can be really quick or ordinary, and then I’m back in it before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Maybe more than I like to admit.\n\nIt’s not always a clear thought like, “This was my fault,” but there’s this feeling that sits there most days, kind of underneath everything. Like a low, sour mix of fear and guilt, even when nothing bad is actually happening right then. I’ll be doing something normal and still feel like I’m carrying this heaviness around, like I should have done something different or handled it better somehow.\n\nAnd I know, logically, maybe that doesn’t make sense. I can tell myself that. But it doesn’t really stop the feeling. It’s just there a lot of the time, kind of coloring the whole day. So yes, guilt is definitely part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t noticed myself moving slowly in a way that I could point to, like someone watching me would say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if I’m just paying more attention to myself now. I’ll catch myself shifting around or not quite settling, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking. So I guess my honest answer is I haven’t clearly noticed a big change either way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear thought like, “I’m going to hurt myself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nThere are moments where I feel worn down or like I don’t want to deal with everything, but I’m not sure that’s the same as wishing I was dead. I don’t think I’ve been making plans or anything like that. It’s more… I get tired of feeling the way I feel, and then I feel guilty for even saying that out loud.\n\nSo, no, not in a direct way. But when you say “better off dead,” I’m not sure… I don’t think I mean that. I haven’t sat there thinking that I should be gone or anything like that.\n\nI guess I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to make it sound better or worse than it is. I feel worn out sometimes, really worn out, but I don’t think I’ve been thinking I’d be better off dead.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like a low, sour mix of fear and guilt, even when nothing bad is actually happening right then. I’ll be doing something normal and still feel like I’m carrying this heaviness around, like I should have done something different or handled it better somehow.\n\nAnd I know, logically, maybe that doesn’t make sense. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it in a clear way, like hours or patterns or anything. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know if it’s enough to call it a real sleep problem.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed that I don’t always feel very rested, but I also can’t say for sure whether that’s because my sleep is bad or because I’m just… worn down in general. I haven’t been paying attention to it the way maybe I should. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, but I also wouldn’t say sleep has felt completely normal either.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the robbery at the festival job, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it, it just sort of barges in. Like if I hear a cash box snap shut — that sharp metal sound — it can bring me right back really fast. Or if I’m in a kitchen and somebody comes up behind me too suddenly, even if they’re just reaching for something or trying to get past me, my whole body reacts before I can think.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m not fully where I am anymore. I can hear the voices again, like the same tone and the same kind of panic, and my stomach just drops. It feels very immediate, like it’s happening right then, even though I know it’s not. And then I feel kind of embarrassed because nothing is actually happening in that moment, but inside it feels like it is. So yes… not constantly, but more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have memories or flashbacks of the robbery that are not triggered by anything specific?\n\nPatient: Not usually out of absolutely nowhere, I don’t think. It’s more like… something sets it off, even if it’s a small thing that maybe wouldn’t mean anything to someone else.\n\nLike I said, the cash box sound is a big one — that snap when it shuts, that sharp metal kind of sound. Or if I’m in a kitchen and someone comes up behind me too fast, even if they’re not doing anything wrong, it’s like my body doesn’t wait to figure that out. It just reacts. And then the robbery comes back really fast, like I can hear the voices again and my stomach drops, and for a second it feels like it’s happening right then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there peacefully and suddenly it appears with no reason at all. It’s more that the reason can be really quick or ordinary, and then I’m back in it before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Maybe more than I like to admit.\n\nIt’s not always a clear thought like, “This was my fault,” but there’s this feeling that sits there most days, kind of underneath everything. Like a low, sour mix of fear and guilt, even when nothing bad is actually happening right then. I’ll be doing something normal and still feel like I’m carrying this heaviness around, like I should have done something different or handled it better somehow.\n\nAnd I know, logically, maybe that doesn’t make sense. I can tell myself that. But it doesn’t really stop the feeling. It’s just there a lot of the time, kind of coloring the whole day. So yes, guilt is definitely part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t noticed myself moving slowly in a way that I could point to, like someone watching me would say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if I’m just paying more attention to myself now. I’ll catch myself shifting around or not quite settling, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking. So I guess my honest answer is I haven’t clearly noticed a big change either way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear thought like, “I’m going to hurt myself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nThere are moments where I feel worn down or like I don’t want to deal with everything, but I’m not sure that’s the same as wishing I was dead. I don’t think I’ve been making plans or anything like that. It’s more… I get tired of feeling the way I feel, and then I feel guilty for even saying that out loud.\n\nSo, no, not in a direct way. But I guess I’m a little unsure where the line is between being really tired of things and what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that thought in that exact way, like, “I’d be better off dead.” That sounds very definite, and I don’t think my mind has gone there clearly.\n\nIt’s more that I get exhausted by how heavy everything feels, and sometimes I wish I could just shut it all off for a while, if that makes sense. But when you say “better off dead,” I’m not sure… I don’t think I mean that. I haven’t sat there thinking that I should be gone or anything like that.\n\nI guess I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to make it sound better or worse than it is. I feel worn out sometimes, really worn out, but I don’t think I’ve been thinking I’d be better off dead.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been fidgeting or having trouble sitting still more often than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do move around some—like I’ll adjust how I’m sitting, cross and uncross my legs, pick at my sleeve or something—but I don’t know that it’s more than usual or enough that I’d call it a problem.\n\nIf someone asked me, “Can you sit through a conversation or sit on the couch without jumping up?” I’d say yes, mostly. I’m not pacing all the time or feeling like I physically can’t stay seated. It’s more that sometimes I notice little restless things, but then I start wondering if I’m only noticing them because we’re talking about it.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve clearly been fidgeting or unable to sit still more than usual. Not in a way I feel sure about.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything, like weighing myself every day, but I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting dramatically different or people commenting that I look like I’ve lost weight.\n\nMaybe my appetite goes up and down a little just like normal, but as far as a significant amount of weight… no, I don’t think I can say that’s happened. If it has changed, it hasn’t been enough that I’ve really noticed or felt worried about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

If it has changed, it hasn’t been enough that I’ve really noticed or felt worried about it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself often feeling the need to be in constant motion?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’d say constant motion, no. I mean, I can get a little restless sometimes, like shifting around or needing to change positions, but it’s not like I feel driven to keep moving all the time.

When I hear “constant motion,” I picture someone pacing around the room or not being able to sit down through a whole conversation, and that doesn’t really feel like me. I can sit. I might fidget a bit or feel uncomfortable for a minute, but I’m not up and down constantly or feeling like I have to move or something bad will happen.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing.

Now they all just feel like things I’m supposed to do because they used to matter to me. I’ll go through the motions, but mostly I’m waiting for it to be over, or I’m thinking about how much effort it took to get there. So… no, I don’t really get much pleasure from them now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything like that. I don’t think there’s been some huge change that I’ve noticed, like my clothes suddenly not fitting or people commenting on it.\n\nThere might be little ups and downs, but nothing where I’d say, “Yes, I lost a lot,” or “Yes, I gained a lot.” It’s just not something I’ve paid a ton of attention to compared to everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it in a clear way, like hours or patterns or anything. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know if it’s enough to call it a real sleep problem.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed that I don’t always feel very rested, but I also can’t say for sure whether that’s because my sleep is bad or because I’m just… worn down in general. I haven’t been paying attention to it the way maybe I should. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, but I also wouldn’t say sleep has felt completely normal either.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the robbery at the festival job, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it, it just sort of barges in. Like if I hear a cash box snap shut — that sharp metal sound — it can bring me right back really fast. Or if I’m in a kitchen and somebody comes up behind me too suddenly, even if they’re just reaching for something or trying to get past me, my whole body reacts before I can think.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m not fully where I am anymore. I can hear the voices again, like the same tone and the same kind of panic, and my stomach just drops. It feels very immediate, like it’s happening right then, even though I know it’s not. And then I feel kind of embarrassed because nothing is actually happening in that moment, but inside it feels like it is. So yes… not constantly, but more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have memories or flashbacks of the robbery that are not triggered by anything specific?\n\nPatient: Not usually out of absolutely nowhere, I don’t think. It’s more like… something sets it off, even if it’s a small thing that maybe wouldn’t mean anything to someone else.\n\nLike I said, the cash box sound is a big one — that snap when it shuts, that sharp metal kind of sound. Or if I’m in a kitchen and someone comes up behind me too fast, even if they’re not doing anything wrong, it’s like my body doesn’t wait to figure that out. It just reacts. And then the robbery comes back really fast, like I can hear the voices again and my stomach drops, and for a second it feels like it’s happening right then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there peacefully and suddenly it appears with no reason at all. It’s more that the reason can be really quick or ordinary, and then I’m back in it before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like a low, sour mix of fear and guilt, even when nothing bad is actually happening right then. I’ll be doing something normal and still feel like I’m carrying this heaviness around, like I should have done something different or handled it better somehow.\n\nAnd I know, logically, maybe that doesn’t make sense. I can tell myself that. But it doesn’t really stop the feeling. It’s just there a lot of the time, kind of coloring the whole day. So yes, guilt is definitely part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t noticed myself moving slowly in a way that I could point to, like someone watching me would say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if I’m just paying more attention to myself now. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I don’t love admitting that, because it makes me feel kind of bad, but yes, I’ve been more short-tempered than I used to be.

It’s not like I’m yelling all day or anything, but little things set me off now in a way that feels out of proportion. Like if my mom asks me something about work, even if she’s just trying to be normal or make conversation, I can feel myself getting irritated right away. Or if someone helping me with something is moving too slow or not understanding what I mean, I get this sharp feeling, and before I’ve really thought it through, I hear myself snapping.

And then right after, I’m usually like, why did I say it like that? It’s like my patience is just thinner. I can tell it’s happening, but in the moment it comes out faster than I can stop it.

Like, not just one thing, but a bunch of things that used to feel like “me,” if that makes sense. Dance class, hiking, trying new recipes, even game night with people I like… they all just feel like chores now, like something sitting on a list that I keep avoiding or putting off. I know I used to look forward to them, and now I just… don’t. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. It started after the robbery at that festival job I had — that was about two years and three months ago, I think. I don’t remember the exact day off the top of my head, but I kind of measure it from that, because before then I still did those things pretty normally. And after that, over time, it was like the interest just drained out of them. So yeah… about two years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since you lost interest in those activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it “sad” exactly all the time. It’s more like there’s this low, sour feeling underneath everything most days. Like fear and guilt mixed together, even when nothing is actually happening and I should be fine.\n\nI’ll be sitting at home or doing something normal, and there’s still this… heaviness, I guess. Not always crying-sad, but not okay either. More like my body and my mind are acting like something is wrong, and then I feel bad about feeling that way, like I should’ve handled things better or I shouldn’t still be like this. It’s there in the background a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you still experience pleasure when you do engage in these activities, even if you don't look forward to them?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least not the way I used to. Sometimes if I make myself go, there might be a tiny moment where I’m like, “Okay, this is fine,” but it doesn’t really feel good in that deeper way anymore.\n\nLike dance class used to make me feel like myself, like I could just be in my body and enjoy moving. Hiking used to feel peaceful, and cooking new recipes felt fun, like a little project. Even game night used to be easy and funny. Now they all just feel like things I’m supposed to do because they used to matter to me. I’ll go through the motions, but mostly I’m waiting for it to be over, or I’m thinking about how much effort it took to get there. So… no, I don’t really get much pleasure from them now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything like that. I don’t think there’s been some huge change that I’ve noticed, like my clothes suddenly not fitting or people commenting on it.\n\nThere might be little ups and downs, but nothing where I’d say, “Yes, I lost a lot,” or “Yes, I gained a lot.” It’s just not something I’ve paid a ton of attention to compared to everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it in a clear way, like hours or patterns or anything. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know if it’s enough to call it a real sleep problem.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed that I don’t always feel very rested, but I also can’t say for sure whether that’s because my sleep is bad or because I’m just… worn down in general. I haven’t been paying attention to it the way maybe I should. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, but I also wouldn’t say sleep has felt completely normal either.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the robbery at the festival job, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it, it just sort of barges in. Like if I hear a cash box snap shut — that sharp metal sound — it can bring me right back really fast. Or if I’m in a kitchen and somebody comes up behind me too suddenly, even if they’re just reaching for something or trying to get past me, my whole body reacts before I can think.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m not fully where I am anymore. I can hear the voices again, like the same tone and the same kind of panic, and my stomach just drops. It feels very immediate, like it’s happening right then, even though I know it’s not. And then I feel kind of embarrassed because nothing is actually happening in that moment, but inside it feels like it is. So yes… not constantly, but more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have memories or flashbacks of the robbery that are not triggered by anything specific?\n\nPatient: Not usually out of absolutely nowhere, I don’t think. It’s more like… something sets it off, even if it’s a small thing that maybe wouldn’t mean anything to someone else.\n\nLike I said, the cash box sound is a big one — that snap when it shuts, that sharp metal kind of sound. Or if I’m in a kitchen and someone comes up behind me too fast, even if they’re not doing anything wrong, it’s like my body doesn’t wait to figure that out. It just reacts. And then the robbery comes back really fast, like I can hear the voices again and my stomach drops, and for a second it feels like it’s happening right then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there peacefully and suddenly it appears with no reason at all. It’s more that the reason can be really quick or ordinary, and then I’m back in it before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Maybe more than I like to admit.\n\nIt’s not always a clear thought like, “This was my fault,” but there’s this feeling that sits there most days, kind of underneath everything. Like a low, sour mix of fear and guilt, even when nothing bad is actually happening right then. I’ll be doing something normal and still feel like I’m carrying this heaviness around, like I should have done something different or handled it better somehow.\n\nAnd I know, logically, maybe that doesn’t make sense. I can tell myself that. But it doesn’t really stop the feeling. It’s just there a lot of the time, kind of coloring the whole day. So yes, guilt is definitely part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t noticed myself moving slowly in a way that I could point to, like someone watching me would say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if I’m just paying more attention to myself now. I’ll catch myself shifting around or not quite settling, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking. So I guess my honest answer is I haven’t clearly noticed a big change either way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear thought like, “I’m going to hurt myself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nThere are moments where I feel worn down or like I don’t want to deal with everything, but I’m not sure that’s the same as wishing I was dead. I don’t think I’ve been making plans or anything like that. It’s more… I get tired of feeling the way I feel, and then I feel guilty for even saying that out loud.\n\nSo, no, not in a direct way. But I guess I’m a little unsure where the line is between being really tired of things and what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that thought in that exact way, like, “I’d be better off dead.” That sounds very definite, and I don’t think my mind has gone there clearly.\n\nIt’s more that I get exhausted by how heavy everything feels, and sometimes I wish I could just shut it all off for a while, if that makes sense. But when you say “better off dead,” I’m not sure… I don’t think I mean that. I haven’t sat there thinking that I should be gone or anything like that.\n\nI guess I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to make it sound better or worse than it is. I feel worn out sometimes, really worn out, but I don’t think I’ve been thinking I’d be better off dead.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been fidgeting or having trouble sitting still more often than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it, it just sort of barges in. Like if I hear a cash box snap shut — that sharp metal sound — it can bring me right back really fast. Or if I’m in a kitchen and somebody comes up behind me too suddenly, even if they’re just reaching for something or trying to get past me, my whole body reacts before I can think.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m not fully where I am anymore. I can hear the voices again, like the same tone and the same kind of panic, and my stomach just drops. It feels very immediate, like it’s happening right then, even though I know it’s not. And then I feel kind of embarrassed because nothing is actually happening in that moment, but inside it feels like it is. So yes… not constantly, but more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have memories or flashbacks of the robbery that are not triggered by anything specific?\n\nPatient: Not usually out of absolutely nowhere, I don’t think. It’s more like… something sets it off, even if it’s a small thing that maybe wouldn’t mean anything to someone else.\n\nLike I said, the cash box sound is a big one — that snap when it shuts, that sharp metal kind of sound. Or if I’m in a kitchen and someone comes up behind me too fast, even if they’re not doing anything wrong, it’s like my body doesn’t wait to figure that out. It just reacts. And then the robbery comes back really fast, like I can hear the voices again and my stomach drops, and for a second it feels like it’s happening right then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there peacefully and suddenly it appears with no reason at all. It’s more that the reason can be really quick or ordinary, and then I’m back in it before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Maybe more than I like to admit.\n\nIt’s not always a clear thought like, “This was my fault,” but there’s this feeling that sits there most days, kind of underneath everything. Like a low, sour mix of fear and guilt, even when nothing bad is actually happening right then. I’ll be doing something normal and still feel like I’m carrying this heaviness around, like I should have done something different or handled it better somehow.\n\nAnd I know, logically, maybe that doesn’t make sense. I can tell myself that. But it doesn’t really stop the feeling. It’s just there a lot of the time, kind of coloring the whole day. So yes, guilt is definitely part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t noticed myself moving slowly in a way that I could point to, like someone watching me would say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if I’m just paying more attention to myself now. I’ll catch myself shifting around or not quite settling, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking. So I guess my honest answer is I haven’t clearly noticed a big change either way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear thought like, “I’m going to hurt myself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nThere are moments where I feel worn down or like I don’t want to deal with everything, but I’m not sure that’s the same as wishing I was dead. I don’t think I’ve been making plans or anything like that. It’s more… I get tired of feeling the way I feel, and then I feel guilty for even saying that out loud.\n\nSo, no, not in a direct way. But I guess I’m a little unsure where the line is between being really tired of things and what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that thought in that exact way, like, “I’d be better off dead.” That sounds very definite, and I don’t think my mind has gone there clearly.\n\nIt’s more that I get exhausted by how heavy everything feels, and sometimes I wish I could just shut it all off for a while, if that makes sense. But when you say “better off dead,” I’m not sure… I don’t think I mean that. I haven’t sat there thinking that I should be gone or anything like that.\n\nI guess I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to make it sound better or worse than it is. I feel worn out sometimes, really worn out, but I don’t think I’ve been thinking I’d be better off dead.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been fidgeting or having trouble sitting still more often than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do move around some—like I’ll adjust how I’m sitting, cross and uncross my legs, pick at my sleeve or something—but I don’t know that it’s more than usual or enough that I’d call it a problem.\n\nIf someone asked me, “Can you sit through a conversation or sit on the couch without jumping up?” I’d say yes, mostly. I’m not pacing all the time or feeling like I physically can’t stay seated. It’s more that sometimes I notice little restless things, but then I start wondering if I’m only noticing them because we’re talking about it.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve clearly been fidgeting or unable to sit still more than usual. Not in a way I feel sure about.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything, like weighing myself every day, but I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting dramatically different or people commenting that I look like I’ve lost weight.\n\nMaybe my appetite goes up and down a little just like normal, but as far as a significant amount of weight… no, I don’t think I can say that’s happened. If it has changed, it hasn’t been enough that I’ve really noticed or felt worried about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself often feeling the need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say constant motion, no. I can sit. Like if my mom asks me something about work, even if she’s just trying to be normal or make conversation, I can feel myself getting irritated right away. Or if someone helping me with something is moving too slow or not understanding what I mean, I get this sharp feeling, and before I’ve really thought it through, I hear myself snapping.\n\nAnd then right after, I’m usually like, why did I say it like that? It’s like my patience is just thinner. I can tell it’s happening, but in the moment it comes out faster than I can stop it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think I’ve been making plans or anything like that. It’s more… I get tired of feeling the way I feel, and then I feel guilty for even saying that out loud.\n\nSo, no, not in a direct way. But I guess I’m a little unsure where the line is between being really tired of things and what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that thought in that exact way, like, “I’d be better off dead.” That sounds very definite, and I don’t think my mind has gone there clearly.\n\nIt’s more that I get exhausted by how heavy everything feels, and sometimes I wish I could just shut it all off for a while, if that makes sense. But when you say “better off dead,” I’m not sure… I don’t think I mean that. I haven’t sat there thinking that I should be gone or anything like that.\n\nI guess I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to make it sound better or worse than it is. I feel worn out sometimes, really worn out, but I don’t think I’ve been thinking I’d be better off dead.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been fidgeting or having trouble sitting still more often than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do move around some—like I’ll adjust how I’m sitting, cross and uncross my legs, pick at my sleeve or something—but I don’t know that it’s more than usual or enough that I’d call it a problem.\n\nIf someone asked me, “Can you sit through a conversation or sit on the couch without jumping up?” I’d say yes, mostly. I’m not pacing all the time or feeling like I physically can’t stay seated. It’s more that sometimes I notice little restless things, but then I start wondering if I’m only noticing them because we’re talking about it.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve clearly been fidgeting or unable to sit still more than usual. Not in a way I feel sure about.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I can tell it’s happening, but in the moment it comes out faster than I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I think about how fragile things are, or I’ll get kind of shaken up by the idea that something bad could happen, but I wouldn’t say I have thoughts about death or dying that keep coming back over and over.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there thinking about dying, or wanting to die, or anything like that. I don’t have a repeated thought that I can’t get out of my head in that way. If it comes up, it’s more like a passing thought, like anyone might have when they’re upset or scared, and then it moves on.\n\nSo, no… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you have less energy than you used to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed that I have less energy, like in a dramatic way. Some days I do feel worn out, but I don’t know if that’s “less energy than before” or just… normal tiredness, or having a lot on my mind, or not sleeping great sometimes. It’s hard for me to separate those things.

Like, I can still do the basic things I need to do. I’m not just lying around all day unable to move. But I guess there are times where I feel kind of drained faster than I expect, especially if I’ve been dealing with people or trying to get through the day and stay calm. Then by the end of it I feel like I don’t have much left.

But if you mean, do I feel physically slowed down or exhausted all the time, no, I don’t think I’d say that. It’s more inconsistent. Some days I feel mostly okay, and other days I’m just tired and kind of done. So… I don’t know if I’d count that as having less energy, exactly.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.8, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.4, F33.8, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I’ve kind of stopped caring about things I used to really enjoy. Like, not just one thing, but a bunch of things that used to feel like “me,” if that makes sense. Dance class, hiking, trying new recipes, even game night with people I like… they all just feel like chores now, like something sitting on a list that I keep avoiding or putting off. I know I used to look forward to them, and now I just… don’t. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now. It started after the robbery at that festival job I had — that was about two years and three months ago, I think. I don’t remember the exact day off the top of my head, but I kind of measure it from that, because before then I still did those things pretty normally. And after that, over time, it was like the interest just drained out of them. So yeah… about two years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since you lost interest in those activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it “sad” exactly all the time. It’s more like there’s this low, sour feeling underneath everything most days. Like fear and guilt mixed together, even when nothing is actually happening and I should be fine.\n\nI’ll be sitting at home or doing something normal, and there’s still this… heaviness, I guess. Not always crying-sad, but not okay either. More like my body and my mind are acting like something is wrong, and then I feel bad about feeling that way, like I should’ve handled things better or I shouldn’t still be like this. It’s there in the background a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you still experience pleasure when you do engage in these activities, even if you don't look forward to them?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least not the way I used to. Sometimes if I make myself go, there might be a tiny moment where I’m like, “Okay, this is fine,” but it doesn’t really feel good in that deeper way anymore.\n\nLike dance class used to make me feel like myself, like I could just be in my body and enjoy moving. Hiking used to feel peaceful, and cooking new recipes felt fun, like a little project. Even game night used to be easy and funny. Now they all just feel like things I’m supposed to do because they used to matter to me. I’ll go through the motions, but mostly I’m waiting for it to be over, or I’m thinking about how much effort it took to get there. So… no, I don’t really get much pleasure from them now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely or anything like that. I don’t think there’s been some huge change that I’ve noticed, like my clothes suddenly not fitting or people commenting on it.\n\nThere might be little ups and downs, but nothing where I’d say, “Yes, I lost a lot,” or “Yes, I gained a lot.” It’s just not something I’ve paid a ton of attention to compared to everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it in a clear way, like hours or patterns or anything. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know if it’s enough to call it a real sleep problem.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed that I don’t always feel very rested, but I also can’t say for sure whether that’s because my sleep is bad or because I’m just… worn down in general. I haven’t been paying attention to it the way maybe I should. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure, but I also wouldn’t say sleep has felt completely normal either.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about the robbery at the festival job, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it, it just sort of barges in. Like if I hear a cash box snap shut — that sharp metal sound — it can bring me right back really fast. Or if I’m in a kitchen and somebody comes up behind me too suddenly, even if they’re just reaching for something or trying to get past me, my whole body reacts before I can think.\n\nIt’s like for a second I’m not fully where I am anymore. I can hear the voices again, like the same tone and the same kind of panic, and my stomach just drops. It feels very immediate, like it’s happening right then, even though I know it’s not. And then I feel kind of embarrassed because nothing is actually happening in that moment, but inside it feels like it is. So yes… not constantly, but more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have memories or flashbacks of the robbery that are not triggered by anything specific?\n\nPatient: Not usually out of absolutely nowhere, I don’t think. It’s more like… something sets it off, even if it’s a small thing that maybe wouldn’t mean anything to someone else.\n\nLike I said, the cash box sound is a big one — that snap when it shuts, that sharp metal kind of sound. Or if I’m in a kitchen and someone comes up behind me too fast, even if they’re not doing anything wrong, it’s like my body doesn’t wait to figure that out. It just reacts. And then the robbery comes back really fast, like I can hear the voices again and my stomach drops, and for a second it feels like it’s happening right then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there peacefully and suddenly it appears with no reason at all. It’s more that the reason can be really quick or ordinary, and then I’m back in it before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about what happened during the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Maybe more than I like to admit.\n\nIt’s not always a clear thought like, “This was my fault,” but there’s this feeling that sits there most days, kind of underneath everything. Like a low, sour mix of fear and guilt, even when nothing bad is actually happening right then. I’ll be doing something normal and still feel like I’m carrying this heaviness around, like I should have done something different or handled it better somehow.\n\nAnd I know, logically, maybe that doesn’t make sense. I can tell myself that. But it doesn’t really stop the feeling. It’s just there a lot of the time, kind of coloring the whole day. So yes, guilt is definitely part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t noticed myself moving slowly in a way that I could point to, like someone watching me would say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if I’m just paying more attention to myself now. I’ll catch myself shifting around or not quite settling, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking. So I guess my honest answer is I haven’t clearly noticed a big change either way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had a clear thought like, “I’m going to hurt myself,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nThere are moments where I feel worn down or like I don’t want to deal with everything, but I’m not sure that’s the same as wishing I was dead. I don’t think I’ve been making plans or anything like that. It’s more… I get tired of feeling the way I feel, and then I feel guilty for even saying that out loud.\n\nSo, no, not in a direct way. But I guess I’m a little unsure where the line is between being really tired of things and what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had that thought in that exact way, like, “I’d be better off dead.” That sounds very definite, and I don’t think my mind has gone there clearly.\n\nIt’s more that I get exhausted by how heavy everything feels, and sometimes I wish I could just shut it all off for a while, if that makes sense. But when you say “better off dead,” I’m not sure… I don’t think I mean that. I haven’t sat there thinking that I should be gone or anything like that.\n\nI guess I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to make it sound better or worse than it is. I feel worn out sometimes, really worn out, but I don’t think I’ve been thinking I’d be better off dead.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been fidgeting or having trouble sitting still more often than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do move around some—like I’ll adjust how I’m sitting, cross and uncross my legs, pick at my sleeve or something—but I don’t know that it’s more than usual or enough that I’d call it a problem.\n\nIf someone asked me, “Can you sit through a conversation or sit on the couch without jumping up?” I’d say yes, mostly. I’m not pacing all the time or feeling like I physically can’t stay seated. It’s more that sometimes I notice little restless things, but then I start wondering if I’m only noticing them because we’re talking about it.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve clearly been fidgeting or unable to sit still more than usual. Not in a way I feel sure about.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or anything, like weighing myself every day, but I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting dramatically different or people commenting that I look like I’ve lost weight.\n\nMaybe my appetite goes up and down a little just like normal, but as far as a significant amount of weight… no, I don’t think I can say that’s happened. If it has changed, it hasn’t been enough that I’ve really noticed or felt worried about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself often feeling the need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say constant motion, no. I mean, I can get a little restless sometimes, like shifting around or needing to change positions, but it’s not like I feel driven to keep moving all the time.\n\nWhen I hear “constant motion,” I picture someone pacing around the room or not being able to sit down through a whole conversation, and that doesn’t really feel like me. I can sit. I might fidget a bit or feel uncomfortable for a minute, but I’m not up and down constantly or feeling like I have to move or something bad will happen.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been more irritable or short-tempered than usual since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love admitting that, because it makes me feel kind of bad, but yes, I’ve been more short-tempered than I used to be.\n\nIt’s not like I’m yelling all day or anything, but little things set me off now in a way that feels out of proportion. Like if my mom asks me something about work, even if she’s just trying to be normal or make conversation, I can feel myself getting irritated right away. Or if someone helping me with something is moving too slow or not understanding what I mean, I get this sharp feeling, and before I’ve really thought it through, I hear myself snapping.\n\nAnd then right after, I’m usually like, why did I say it like that? It’s like my patience is just thinner. I can tell it’s happening, but in the moment it comes out faster than I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I think about how fragile things are, or I’ll get kind of shaken up by the idea that something bad could happen, but I wouldn’t say I have thoughts about death or dying that keep coming back over and over.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there thinking about dying, or wanting to die, or anything like that. I don’t have a repeated thought that I can’t get out of my head in that way. If it comes up, it’s more like a passing thought, like anyone might have when they’re upset or scared, and then it moves on.\n\nSo, no… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less energy than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed that I have less energy, like in a dramatic way. Some days I do feel worn out, but I don’t know if that’s “less energy than before” or just… normal tiredness, or having a lot on my mind, or not sleeping great sometimes. It’s hard for me to separate those things.\n\nLike, I can still do the basic things I need to do. I’m not just lying around all day unable to move. But I guess there are times where I feel kind of drained faster than I expect, especially if I’ve been dealing with people or trying to get through the day and stay calm. Then by the end of it I feel like I don’t have much left.\n\nBut if you mean, do I feel physically slowed down or exhausted all the time, no, I don’t think I’d say that. It’s more inconsistent. Some days I feel mostly okay, and other days I’m just tired and kind of done. So… I don’t know if I’d count that as having less energy, exactly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

If it has changed, it hasn’t been enough that I’ve really noticed or felt worried about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself often feeling the need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say constant motion, no. I mean, I can get a little restless sometimes, like shifting around or needing to change positions, but it’s not like I feel driven to keep moving all the time.\n\nWhen I hear “constant motion,” I picture someone pacing around the room or not being able to sit down through a whole conversation, and that doesn’t really feel like me. I can sit. I might fidget a bit or feel uncomfortable for a minute, but I’m not up and down constantly or feeling like I have to move or something bad will happen.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been more irritable or short-tempered than usual since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so.
